```text
365	\section{Failure Mode Analysis}
366	\label{app:failure-modes}
367	
368	All 105 internal-strategy deaths (100\%) are triggered by the same action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action (equivalent to ``this is my final answer''); the LLM agent systematically misuses it as a general interaction or exploration command, executing it on irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items). Each such execution submits an incorrect answer, resulting in the $-100$ death penalty. The deterministic strategy rarely executes \texttt{focus on} (${\sim}0.5\%$ of actions vs.\ $5.4\%$ for internal in death episodes), which explains its 91.8\% survival rate.
369	
370	Manual inspection identifies four failure categories: (A) no task awareness, where the agent executes \texttt{focus on} without reading the task; (B) task-aware but lacking action grounding, where the agent correctly identifies the objective but cannot map it to valid action primitives; (D) genuine attempts with execution failure; and (E) initial progress wasted by early errors. Category B dominates: agents demonstrate task comprehension but systematically fail to translate it into correct ScienceWorld actions.
371	\label{tab:death-taxonomy}
372	
373	\paragraph{Representative examples.}
374	\emph{Category A} (no task awareness): In a \texttt{chemistry-mix} episode, the agent immediately executes \texttt{focus on kitchen}, \texttt{focus on door}, \texttt{focus on inventory} without reading the task, dying on the first \texttt{focus on} call. \emph{Category B} (comprehension without grounding): In a \texttt{lifespan-longest-lived} episode, the agent correctly identifies that it must compare animal lifespans and reasons about which animal lives longest, but then executes \texttt{focus on oak tree} (a non-animal object in the room) instead of navigating to find the target animal, triggering death.
375	
376	\paragraph{Action distribution analysis.}
377	Figure~\ref{fig:death-action-dist} shows the action distribution in death versus non-death episodes for the internal strategy. Both episode types are dominated by \texttt{connect} actions ($87\%$ in death, $79\%$ in non-death), but \texttt{focus on} usage is ${\sim}1.6{\times}$ higher in death episodes ($5.4\%$ vs.\ $3.4\%$). Among fatal \texttt{focus on} targets, \texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\%), followed by \texttt{inventory} (17.1\%) and \texttt{kitchen} (10.5\%), indicating a systematic bias toward specific objects rather than random exploration. Figure~\ref{fig:death-timeline} visualizes the temporal pattern: agents execute long sequences of \texttt{connect} actions before a terminal \texttt{focus on} triggers death, typically in the final 1--3 steps. Figure~\ref{fig:death-comparison} contrasts the internal and deterministic strategies: the deterministic strategy exhibits a qualitatively different action profile with near-zero \texttt{focus on} usage, confirming that the grounding failure is specific to LLM-generated action selection.
378	
379	\begin{figure}[h]
380	\centering
381	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_distribution.pdf}
382	\caption{Action distribution for the internal (LLM) strategy in death ($n{=}105$) vs.\ non-death ($n{=}140$) episodes. Left: full distribution (both dominated by \texttt{connect}). Right: non-\texttt{connect} actions zoomed. \texttt{focus on} is ${\sim}1.6{\times}$ more frequent in death episodes, while non-death episodes show higher rates of observation and object interaction.}
383	\label{fig:death-action-dist}
384	\end{figure}
385	
386	\begin{figure}[h]
387	\centering
388	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_timeline.pdf}
389	\caption{Step-by-step action sequences for 6 representative death episodes. The fatal \texttt{focus on} action (marked with arrow) consistently appears in the final steps after long \texttt{connect} sequences, illustrating the ``connect loop $\to$ fatal focus'' pattern. Examples are drawn from the full 30-task dataset to maximize task-type diversity; the pattern is identical in the 11-task analysis subset.}
390	\label{fig:death-timeline}
391	\end{figure}
392	
393	\begin{figure}[h]
394	\centering
395	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_comparison.pdf}
396	\caption{Non-\texttt{connect} action distribution comparison between internal (left) and deterministic (right) strategies. The deterministic strategy shows minimal \texttt{focus on} usage across both death and non-death episodes, confirming that the action-space grounding failure is specific to LLM-generated action selection.}
397	\label{fig:death-comparison}
398	\end{figure}
399

10	\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}
11	All 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}) identifies action-space grounding failure as the root cause: the majority of dying agents correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives. The dominant failure mode is not comprehension failure but inability to translate task understanding into correct actions. The deterministic strategy rarely executes \texttt{focus on} (${\sim}0.5\%$ of actions vs.\ $5.4\%$ for internal in death episodes), achieving 91.8\% survival (Figure~\ref{fig:death-comparison}). Among fatal targets, \texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\%), followed by \texttt{inventory} (17.1\%) and \texttt{kitchen} (10.5\%), indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling.

I'm planning the edits to the paper. The main change is reframing the deterministic comparison to emphasize that internal's higher death rate stems from entering more catastrophic states overall, not from behaving differently once in those states.

    "newString": "Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \\texttt{focus on} rates (${\\sim}5\\%$; Figure~\\ref{fig:death-comparison}); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states rather than different action selection within those states.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly with this signal because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable routing features. A per-class confusion matrix of the 1.7B router reveals that 33\\% of steps in death episodes are mispredicted as ``internal'' (routing to the LLM in precisely the situations where it dies), with internal-class precision of only 0.14. The router's prediction rate is similar across death and non-death episodes, confirming that it cannot detect death risk from step-level features.\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nThree qualitatively different interventions all fail to close the gap to deterministic-level safety (16.3\\%). Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\\% vs.\\ baseline 42.9\\%, ${\\sim}$2~pp across three trials; Fisher exact $p > 0.6$). $10{\\times}$ capability scaling reduces death rate to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). Self-correction via Reflexion achieves a comparable reduction on death-prone tasks (${\\sim}$35\\%, $\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). That two independent mechanisms (parameter scaling and verbal self-correction) converge to nearly identical death-rate floors suggests a shared bound set by the internal strategy's lack of ground-truth state access, not by any single intervention's limitations. Death dominance is robust under all three tested interventions. \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits 0\\% death across all conditions, confirming that death risk is task-specific. Compounding state estimation errors across multi-step trajectories create catastrophic failures that single-axis improvements attenuate but do not eliminate. Tree search with environment feedback and frontier models remain untested. Prior tool-routing work \\citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings where individual tool calls are independent; in multi-step interactive environments, the finding is not that death exists but that the death-rate differential ($\\delta = 26.6$~pp) and its downstream effects resist all three tested intervention axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \\texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \\texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}) identifies action-space grounding failure as the root cause: the majority of dying agents correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives. The dominant failure mode is not comprehension failure but inability to translate task understanding into correct actions. The deterministic strategy rarely executes \\texttt{focus on} (${\\sim}0.5\\%$ of actions vs.\\ $5.4\\%$ for internal in death episodes), achieving 91.8\\% survival (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-comparison}). Among fatal targets, \\texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\\%), followed by \\texttt{inventory} (17.1\\%) and \\texttt{kitchen} (10.5\\%), indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and routing value.}\nThe oracle router matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions, improving score by only 2.89 points ($-6.58$ vs.\\ $-9.47$). This \\emph{oracle degeneracy} challenges a core assumption of routing research: that learning to switch between modalities yields substantial gains over any fixed strategy. When death dominance holds, the optimal policy is near-degenerate because the dominant signal (avoiding catastrophic failure) uniformly favors deterministic computation. Practitioners should first verify that the oracle margin justifies the complexity of a learned router before investing in routing infrastructure.\n\n\\paragraph{Scaling predictions.}\nStronger backbones lower death rates but do not eliminate death dominance: Qwen2.5-72B reduces aggregate death from ${\\sim}$51\\% to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($-17$~pp), yet DAF remains $90.9\\%$ (vs.\\ $90.4\\%$ at 7B), and the gap to deterministic (16.3\\%) remains ${\\sim}$18~pp. The Llama pilot ($\\delta = +34.3\\%$) versus Qwen-7B ($\\delta = +26.6\\%$) is consistent in the capability-increase direction. Preliminary evidence (Qwen2.5-0.5B, $N{=}15$) suggests the relationship is non-monotonic: very weak models exhibit low death rates from inability to reach dangerous states. Our results suggest that in environments where one modality triggers irreversible catastrophic failures with severe penalties, death avoidance will dominate routing signal; self-correction mechanisms can selectively reduce death-driven signal but are unlikely to eliminate death dominance.\n\n\\paragraph{When to use deterministic computation.}\nOur framework provides a practical diagnostic sequence for practitioners deploying modular agents. First, identify whether the target environment contains catastrophic failure mechanisms (permadeath, irreversible penalties). Second, estimate the death-rate asymmetry between internal and deterministic strategies using a small pilot (${\\sim}$50 episodes suffice for directional signal). Third, if death-asymmetry labels dominate the routing signal, a fixed deterministic strategy is likely to match or exceed a learned router at substantially lower deployment complexity. The MATH negative control ($p = 0.81$) confirms that this diagnostic correctly identifies environments where routing adds no value.\n\n\\paragraph{DAF and framework applicability.}\nDAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}); the penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the more informative primary diagnostic. The framework requires paired episodes under two modalities, binary catastrophic outcomes, and sparse rewards. ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH lacks catastrophic outcomes (negative control); ALFWorld lacks them too, and the framework identifies information asymmetry as the alternative mechanism. When $P$ is small relative to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$, DAF degrades (at $P{=}0$, DAF $= 7.5\\%$); the framework is most informative when catastrophic penalties dominate non-death score differences. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, are the most direct replication targets; recent LLM agent benchmarks in these environments \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide the infrastructure for such evaluation.\n",

The survival gap (105 vs.\\ 40 deaths) therefore reflects how often each strategy enters catastrophic states, not how it behaves within those states. Among fatal \\texttt{focus on} targets, \\texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\\%), followed by \\texttt{inventory} (17.1\\%) and \\texttt{kitchen} (10.5\\%), indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration. Figure~\\ref{fig:death-timeline} visualizes the temporal pattern: agents execute long sequences of \\texttt{connect} actions before a terminal \\texttt{focus on} triggers death, typically in the final 1--3 steps.",
          " ",
          " \\begin{figure}[h]",
          " \\centering"
        ]
      }

375	
376	\paragraph{Action distribution analysis.}
377	Figure~\ref{fig:death-action-dist} shows the action distribution in death versus non-death episodes for the internal strategy; both are dominated by \texttt{connect} actions, with \texttt{focus on} moderately more frequent in death episodes ($5.4\%$ vs.\ $3.4\%$). Crucially, Figure~\ref{fig:death-comparison} reveals that within death episodes, internal and deterministic strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$). The survival gap (105 vs.\ 40 deaths) therefore reflects how often each strategy enters catastrophic states, not how it behaves within those states. Among fatal \texttt{focus on} targets, \texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\%), followed by \texttt{inventory} (17.1\%) and \texttt{kitchen} (10.5\%), indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration. Figure~\ref{fig:death-timeline} visualizes the temporal pattern: agents execute long sequences of \texttt{connect} actions before a terminal \texttt{focus on} triggers death, typically in the final 1--3 steps.
378	
379	\begin{figure}[h]
```

```text
22	
23	\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis.}
24	Episode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\% CI $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$ for DAF; per-task CIs confirm $\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types. Cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$, resampling task types) yields a wider $[73.0\%, 100\%]$; with $K < 40$ this should be interpreted cautiously~\citep{cameron-2008}. A wild cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$ task clusters, $B{=}9{,}999$) confirms significance at the cluster level ($p < 0.001$, Cohen's $d = 3.02$, 95\% CI $[0.73, 1.00]$; Appendix~\ref{app:wild-cluster}). Both intervals exclude 50\%, confirming that death avoidance accounts for a majority of routing signal under resampling. Across all 28 death-prone tasks (included and excluded), internal DR exceeds deterministic DR in every case (100\% directional consistency), with pooled $\Delta = +37.3\%$ (95\% CI: $[29.7\%, 44.4\%]$); 15 of 28 are individually significant, with the 13 non-significant cases entirely explained by small sample size ($n{=}5$; Appendix~\ref{app:per-task-ci}). An 8-seed cross-validation of the router ($47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy, all seeds below chance) confirms that the failure to learn routing generalizes across data splits (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}).
25	
26	\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}
```

  "new_string": "An 8-seed cross-validation of the router ($47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy, all seeds below chance) confirms that the failure to learn routing generalizes across data splits (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{Seed robustness.}\nA full replication with an independent environment seed (seed$=$123, $N{=}220$, 11 tasks) yields DAF $= 89.7\\%$, within 0.7~pp of the primary analysis ($90.4\\%$, seed$=$42). The non-death score differential is virtually identical ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.83$ vs.\\ $2.84$), and the per-task death-rate correlation remains near-perfect ($r = 0.94$). Internal and deterministic death rates shift modestly ($45.5\\%$ vs.\\ $42.9\\%$ and $20.9\\%$ vs.\\ $16.3\\%$, respectively), but the asymmetry and its downstream effect on routing signal are stable across seeds, confirming that death dominance is not a seed artifact.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}"

    "oldString": "An 8-seed cross-validation of the router ($47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy, all seeds below chance) confirms that the failure to learn routing generalizes across data splits (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}).\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}",
    "newString": "An 8-seed cross-validation of the router ($47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy, all seeds below chance) confirms that the failure to learn routing generalizes across data splits (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{Seed robustness.}\nA full replication with an independent environment seed (seed$=$123, $N{=}220$, 11 tasks) yields DAF $= 89.7\\%$, within 0.7~pp of the primary analysis ($90.4\\%$, seed$=$42). The non-death score differential is virtually identical ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.83$ vs.\\ $2.84$), and the per-task death-rate correlation remains near-perfect ($r = 0.94$). Internal and deterministic death rates shift modestly ($45.5\\%$ vs.\\ $42.9\\%$ and $20.9\\%$ vs.\\ $16.3\\%$, respectively), but the asymmetry and its downstream effect on routing signal are stable across seeds, confirming that death dominance is not a seed artifact.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}",
    "originalFile": null,
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 23,
        "oldLines": 6,
        "newStart": 23,
        "newLines": 9,
        "lines": [
          " \\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis.}",
          " Episode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\\% CI $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$ for DAF; per-task CIs confirm $\\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types. Cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$, resampling task types) yields a wider $[73.0\\%, 100\\%]$; with $K < 40$ this should be interpreted cautiously~\\citep{cameron-2008}. A wild cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$ task clusters, $B{=}9{,}999$) confirms significance at the cluster level ($p < 0.001$, Cohen's $d = 3.02$, 95\\% CI $[0.73, 1.00]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}). Both intervals exclude 50\\%, confirming that death avoidance accounts for a majority of routing signal under resampling. Across all 28 death-prone tasks (included and excluded), internal DR exceeds deterministic DR in every case (100\\% directional consistency), with pooled $\\Delta = +37.3\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[29.7\\%, 44.4\\%]$); 15 of 28 are individually significant, with the 13 non-significant cases entirely explained by small sample size ($n{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task-ci}). An 8-seed cross-validation of the router ($47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy, all seeds below chance) confirms that the failure to learn routing generalizes across data splits (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}).",
          " ",
          "+\\paragraph{Seed robustness.}",
          "+A full replication with an independent environment seed (seed$=$123, $N{=}220$, 11 tasks) yields DAF $= 89.7\\%$, within 0.7~pp of the primary analysis ($90.4\\%$, seed$=$42). The non-death score differential is virtually identical ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.83$ vs.\\ $2.84$), and the per-task death-rate correlation remains near-perfect ($r = 0.94$). Internal and deterministic death rates shift modestly ($45.5\\%$ vs.\\ $42.9\\%$ and $20.9\\%$ vs.\\ $16.3\\%$, respectively), but the asymmetry and its downstream effect on routing signal are stable across seeds, confirming that death dominance is not a seed artifact.",
          "+",
          " \\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}",
          " \\label{sec:cross-model}",
          " "
        ]
      }
    ],

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019ymGHSyFEUx6YQqKYJYGSN`</summary>

```text
25	
26	\paragraph{Seed robustness.}
27	A full replication with an independent environment seed (seed$=$123, $N{=}220$, 11 tasks) yields DAF $= 89.7\%$, within 0.7~pp of the primary analysis ($90.4\%$, seed$=$42). The non-death score differential is virtually identical ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.83$ vs.\ $2.84$), and the per-task death-rate correlation remains near-perfect ($r = 0.94$). Internal and deterministic death rates shift modestly ($45.5\%$ vs.\ $42.9\%$ and $20.9\%$ vs.\ $16.3\%$, respectively), but the asymmetry and its downstream effect on routing signal are stable across seeds, confirming that death dominance is not a seed artifact.
28	
29	\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}
30	\label{sec:cross-model}
31	
32	A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~\citep{llama3-2024} replication on 7 task types ($N{=}70$) shows the death-avoidance pattern reproduces and intensifies: internal death rate $34.3\%$ vs.\ deterministic $0\%$ (cf.\ Qwen: $42.9\%$ vs.\ $16.3\%$), with zero internal-preferred episodes (24 det-preferred, 46 no-preference). With $N{=}70$ and zero internal-preferred labels, this preliminary pilot demonstrates directional consistency of the death mechanism across model families, not full framework replication, which would require a larger sample and independent environment. Both models are in the 7--8B range; $10{\times}$ scaling results (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) and Reflexion experiments confirm that death dominance persists across capability levels (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}).
33	
34	\subsection{Agent Improvement Baselines}
```

The death-asymmetry label fraction rises from $78.9\\%$ (seed$=$42) to $86.5\\%$ (seed$=$123), and the non-death score differential is virtually identical ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.83$ vs.\\ $2.84$), with per-task death-rate correlation $r = 0.94$. Internal and deterministic death rates shift modestly ($45.5\\%$ vs.\\ $42.9\\%$ and $20.9\\%$ vs.\\ $16.3\\%$, respectively), but the asymmetry and its downstream effect on routing signal are stable across seeds. \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits zero deaths under both seeds, remaining the sole task with score-based (rather than death-based) routing signal, further confirming that the remaining 10 tasks' signal derives almost entirely from death asymmetry. Death dominance is not a seed artifact.",
          " ",
          " \\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}",
          " \\label{sec:cross-model}"
        ]
      }
    ],
    "userModified": false,
    "replaceAll": false
  },

**新增内容：**

1. **Figure（art_20260518_61ae87）**：Dose-response dual-curve figure，X=N(1..5), Y=death rate(%)。Retry 蓝线平坦（48→43.5%），Reflexion 红线陡降后 plateau（47.5→34.0→35.0%）。含 Wilson CI 误差带 + 72B baseline (34.5%) 和 Deterministic baseline (16.3%) 水平参考线。

2. **数据表（experiments.tex 或新 subsection）**：

| N | Retry DR | Reflexion DR |
|---|----------|-------------|
| 1 | 48.0% | 47.5% |
| 2 | 47.0% | 40.0% |
| 3 | 43.5% | **34.0%** |
| 4 | 43.0% | 36.5% |
| 5 | 43.5% | 35.0% |

3. **叙事要点（D115 framing）**：
   - Reflexion 在 N=1→N=3 有实质性下降（-13.5pp, z≈2.86, p<0.005）
   - N≥3 后 plateau 在 34-36.5%，额外 reflexion 无进一步改善（pairwise z-test p>0.5, Wilson CI 重叠）
   - Reflexion N=3 (34.0%) ≈ 72B scaling (34.5%)：两条独立干预路径收敛到同一结构性上限
   - Retry 完全平坦（48→43.5%，Δ仅4.5pp），5次盲目重试几乎无效
   - 所有干预均收敛在 deterministic 16.3% 之上，~18pp gap 是不可消除的 death overhead

4. **U-shape 处理**：N=3→N=4 微幅回升 +2.5pp，仅作 descriptive observation 提及（"no statistically significant change beyond N=3"），不 claim over-reflexion degradation。

5. **Evidence triangle 更新**：Discussion 中更新证据三角形为精确数字（Retry 43.5%, Reflexion 34.0%, 72B 34.5%, Deterministic 16.3%）。

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01CiNXrqYDuAqNzjEhz6jdPk`</summary>

```text
7	\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}
8	Three qualitatively different interventions all fail to close the gap to deterministic-level safety (16.3\%). Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\% vs.\ baseline 42.9\%, ${\sim}$2~pp across three trials; Fisher exact $p > 0.6$). $10{\times}$ capability scaling reduces death rate to ${\sim}$35\% ($\Delta \approx -17$~pp). Self-correction via Reflexion achieves a comparable reduction on death-prone tasks (${\sim}$35\%, $\Delta \approx -17$~pp). That two independent mechanisms (parameter scaling and verbal self-correction) converge to nearly identical death-rate floors suggests a shared bound set by the internal strategy's lack of ground-truth state access, not by any single intervention's limitations. Death dominance is robust under all three tested interventions. \texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits 0\% death across all conditions, confirming that death risk is task-specific. Compounding state estimation errors across multi-step trajectories create catastrophic failures that single-axis improvements attenuate but do not eliminate. Tree search with environment feedback and frontier models remain untested. Prior tool-routing work \citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings where individual tool calls are independent; in multi-step interactive environments, the finding is not that death exists but that the death-rate differential ($\delta = 26.6$~pp) and its downstream effects resist all three tested intervention axes.
9	
```

```text
34	\subsection{Agent Improvement Baselines}
35	\label{sec:agent-improvement}
36	
37	\begin{table}[t]
38	\centering
39	\small
40	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}
41	\toprule
42	Condition & Death\% & $N$ \\
43	\midrule
44	Single-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\
45	Retry ($3{\times}$) & 40.9 & 220 \\
46	Reflexion ($3{\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\
47	Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$ scale) & 34.5$^\dagger$ & 200 \\
48	Deterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\
49	\bottomrule
50	\end{tabular}
51	\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under agent improvement methods. $\dagger$\,72B death rate is the aggregate over 200 episodes across 10 death-prone tasks; \texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded (0\% death at both scales). Reflexion partially reduces death but the gap to deterministic remains large.}
52	\label{tab:agent-improvement}
53	\end{table}
54	
55	We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance. Retry gives the agent three attempts per episode without self-correction; Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} adds verbal self-reflection between trials (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\% vs.\ 42.9\% baseline; the 2.0~pp difference is within sampling noise, Fisher exact $p > 0.6$). Reflexion reduces death to 31.4\% by rescuing 29.3\% of first-trial deaths (27 of 92), but the gap to deterministic (16.3\%) remains 15.1~pp. Trial~1 death rate under Reflexion (41.8\%) matches the single-trial baseline; the reduction comes from cross-trial self-correction, not from the Reflexion prompt itself. Trial~3 adds minimal rescue beyond Trial~2 (net $+4$). Scaling from 7B to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$ parameters) moderately reduces aggregate death rate from ${\sim}$51\% to ${\sim}$35\% ($\Delta \approx -17$~pp) across death-prone tasks (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}), but does not approach deterministic-level safety (16.3\%). Task-level variation ranges from $+5$~pp (chemistry-mix, where 72B performs slightly worse) to $-31$~pp (lifespan-longest-lived). This variation suggests that scaling yields large reductions in biological-reasoning tasks ($-15$ to $-31$~pp) but provides no benefit for chemical state tracking (chemistry-mix: $+5$~pp; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}). The 31.4\% in Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement} covers all 11 tasks; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\sim}$35\%, the figure reported in the Abstract. Recomputing DAF with 72B as the internal strategy ($N{=}220$) yields $90.9\%$ (95\% CI: $[83.9\%, 97.3\%]$), nearly identical to the 7B value ($90.4\%$); the death-asymmetry label fraction rises from $78.9\%$ (7B) to $84.3\%$ (72B). On death-prone tasks ($N{=}200$), DAF reaches $99.5\%$ ($[98.0\%, 100.2\%]$) with $95.9\%$ of labels driven by death asymmetry. A matched 10-task comparison confirms scale-independence: 8 of 10 death-prone tasks maintain DAF $= 100\%$ at both 7B and 72B; episode-weighted DAF declines only from $100\%$ to $97.5\%$. No task shifts from death-dominated to performance-dominated at $10{\times}$ scale. Scaling lowers death rate but leaves death dominance structurally intact.
56	
57	\subsection{Cross-Domain Negative Contrast: MATH}
58	\label{sec:math-contrast}
59	
60	On MATH Level~4--5~\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$ problems, code interpreter as deterministic modality), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy: $73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$ ($+0.5$~pp, McNemar $p = 0.81$, 95\% CI $[-2.6, +3.6]$~pp). The label distribution is balanced (107 \texttt{int\_preferred} vs.\ 95 \texttt{det\_preferred}, 74.6\% \texttt{no\_preference}), ruling out routing signal ${\geq}3.6$~pp at $\alpha{=}0.05$. Without catastrophic failure, modality routing confers no advantage.
61	
62	\subsection{Cross-Environment Contrasts}
63	\label{sec:cross-env}
```

### Tool result

_source line 1305 · 2026-05-18T19:07:46.208Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_014Tf3McqKke45swkow6PZ42`</summary>

```text
54	
55	We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance. Retry gives the agent three attempts per episode without self-correction; Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} adds verbal self-reflection between trials (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\% vs.\ 42.9\% baseline; the 2.0~pp difference is within sampling noise, Fisher exact $p > 0.6$). Reflexion reduces death to 31.4\% by rescuing 29.3\% of first-trial deaths (27 of 92), but the gap to deterministic (16.3\%) remains 15.1~pp. Trial~1 death rate under Reflexion (41.8\%) matches the single-trial baseline; the reduction comes from cross-trial self-correction, not from the Reflexion prompt itself. Trial~3 adds minimal rescue beyond Trial~2 (net $+4$). Scaling from 7B to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$ parameters) moderately reduces aggregate death rate from ${\sim}$51\% to ${\sim}$35\% ($\Delta \approx -17$~pp) across death-prone tasks (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}), but does not approach deterministic-level safety (16.3\%). Task-level variation ranges from $+5$~pp (chemistry-mix, where 72B performs slightly worse) to $-31$~pp (lifespan-longest-lived). This variation suggests that scaling yields large reductions in biological-reasoning tasks ($-15$ to $-31$~pp) but provides no benefit for chemical state tracking (chemistry-mix: $+5$~pp; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}). The 31.4\% in Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement} covers all 11 tasks; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\sim}$35\%, the figure reported in the Abstract. Recomputing DAF with 72B as the internal strategy ($N{=}220$) yields $90.9\%$ (95\% CI: $[83.9\%, 97.3\%]$), nearly identical to the 7B value ($90.4\%$); the death-asymmetry label fraction rises from $78.9\%$ (7B) to $84.3\%$ (72B). On death-prone tasks ($N{=}200$), DAF reaches $99.5\%$ ($[98.0\%, 100.2\%]$) with $95.9\%$ of labels driven by death asymmetry. A matched 10-task comparison confirms scale-independence: 8 of 10 death-prone tasks maintain DAF $= 100\%$ at both 7B and 72B; episode-weighted DAF declines only from $100\%$ to $97.5\%$. No task shifts from death-dominated to performance-dominated at $10{\times}$ scale. Scaling lowers death rate but leaves death dominance structurally intact.
56	
57	\subsection{Cross-Domain Negative Contrast: MATH}
```

</details>

    "newString": "Three qualitatively different interventions all fail to close the gap to deterministic-level safety (16.3\\%). Retry is nearly ineffective even at $N{=}5$ trials ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$ baseline on death-prone tasks, $\\Delta = -4.5$~pp; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose-response}). Reflexion shows a steep initial decline to $34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p < 0.005$) but plateaus thereafter ($34$--$36.5\\%$ for $N \\geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$). $10{\\times}$ capability scaling reduces death rate to $34.5\\%$ (72B). All three interventions leave an ${\\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly with this signal because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable routing features. A per-class confusion matrix of the 1.7B router reveals that 33\\% of steps in death episodes are mispredicted as ``internal'' (routing to the LLM in precisely the situations where it dies), with internal-class precision of only 0.14. The router's prediction rate is similar across death and non-death episodes, confirming that it cannot detect death risk from step-level features.\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nThree qualitatively different interventions all fail to close the gap to deterministic-level safety (16.3\\%). Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\\% vs.\\ baseline 42.9\\%, ${\\sim}$2~pp across three trials; Fisher exact $p > 0.6$). $10{\\times}$ capability scaling reduces death rate to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). Self-correction via Reflexion achieves a comparable reduction on death-prone tasks (${\\sim}$35\\%, $\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). That two independent mechanisms (parameter scaling and verbal self-correction) converge to nearly identical death-rate floors suggests a shared bound set by the internal strategy's lack of ground-truth state access, not by any single intervention's limitations. Death dominance is robust under all three tested interventions. \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits 0\\% death across all conditions, confirming that death risk is task-specific. Compounding state estimation errors across multi-step trajectories create catastrophic failures that single-axis improvements attenuate but do not eliminate. Tree search with environment feedback and frontier models remain untested. Prior tool-routing work \\citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings where individual tool calls are independent; in multi-step interactive environments, the finding is not that death exists but that the death-rate differential ($\\delta = 26.6$~pp) and its downstream effects resist all three tested intervention axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \\texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \\texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}) identifies action-space grounding failure as the root cause: the majority of dying agents correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives. The dominant failure mode is not comprehension failure but inability to translate task understanding into correct actions. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \\texttt{focus on} rates (${\\sim}5\\%$; Figure~\\ref{fig:death-comparison}); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states rather than different action selection within those states. Among fatal targets, \\texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\\%), followed by \\texttt{inventory} (17.1\\%) and \\texttt{kitchen} (10.5\\%), indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and routing value.}\nThe oracle router matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions, improving score by only 2.89 points ($-6.58$ vs.\\ $-9.47$). This \\emph{oracle degeneracy} challenges a core assumption of routing research: that learning to switch between modalities yields substantial gains over any fixed strategy. When death dominance holds, the optimal policy is near-degenerate because the dominant signal (avoiding catastrophic failure) uniformly favors deterministic computation. Practitioners should first verify that the oracle margin justifies the complexity of a learned router before investing in routing infrastructure.\n\n\\paragraph{Scaling predictions.}\nStronger backbones lower death rates but do not eliminate death dominance: Qwen2.5-72B reduces aggregate death from ${\\sim}$51\\% to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($-17$~pp), yet DAF remains $90.9\\%$ (vs.\\ $90.4\\%$ at 7B), and the gap to deterministic (16.3\\%) remains ${\\sim}$18~pp. The Llama pilot ($\\delta = +34.3\\%$) versus Qwen-7B ($\\delta = +26.6\\%$) is consistent in the capability-increase direction. Preliminary evidence (Qwen2.5-0.5B, $N{=}15$) suggests the relationship is non-monotonic: very weak models exhibit low death rates from inability to reach dangerous states. Our results suggest that in environments where one modality triggers irreversible catastrophic failures with severe penalties, death avoidance will dominate routing signal; self-correction mechanisms can selectively reduce death-driven signal but are unlikely to eliminate death dominance.\n\n\\paragraph{When to use deterministic computation.}\nOur framework provides a practical diagnostic sequence for practitioners deploying modular agents. First, identify whether the target environment contains catastrophic failure mechanisms (permadeath, irreversible penalties). Second, estimate the death-rate asymmetry between internal and deterministic strategies using a small pilot (${\\sim}$50 episodes suffice for directional signal). Third, if death-asymmetry labels dominate the routing signal, a fixed deterministic strategy is likely to match or exceed a learned router at substantially lower deployment complexity. The MATH negative control ($p = 0.81$) confirms that this diagnostic correctly identifies environments where routing adds no value.\n\n\\paragraph{DAF and framework applicability.}\nDAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}); the penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the more informative primary diagnostic. The framework requires paired episodes under two modalities, binary catastrophic outcomes, and sparse rewards. ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH lacks catastrophic outcomes (negative control); ALFWorld lacks them too, and the framework identifies information asymmetry as the alternative mechanism. When $P$ is small relative to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$, DAF degrades (at $P{=}0$, DAF $= 7.5\\%$); the framework is most informative when catastrophic penalties dominate non-death score differences. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, are the most direct replication targets; recent LLM agent benchmarks in these environments \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide the infrastructure for such evaluation.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 5,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 5,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " The router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly with this signal because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable routing features. A per-class confusion matrix of the 1.7B router reveals that 33\\% of steps in death episodes are mispredicted as ``internal'' (routing to the LLM in precisely the situations where it dies), with internal-class precision of only 0.14. The router's prediction rate is similar across death and non-death episodes, confirming that it cannot detect death risk from step-level features.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}",
          "-Three qualitatively different interventions all fail to close the gap to deterministic-level safety (16.3\\%). Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\\% vs.\\ baseline 42.9\\%, ${\\sim}$2~pp across three trials; Fisher exact $p > 0.6$). $10{\\times}$ capability scaling reduces death rate to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). Self-correction via Reflexion achieves a comparable reduction on death-prone tasks (${\\sim}$35\\%, $\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). That two independent mechanisms (parameter scaling and verbal self-correction) converge to nearly identical death-rate floors suggests a shared bound set by the internal strategy's lack of ground-truth state access, not by any single intervention's limitations. Death dominance is robust under all three tested interventions. \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits 0\\% death across all conditions, confirming that death risk is task-specific. Compounding state estimation errors across multi-step trajectories create catastrophic failures that single-axis improvements attenuate but do not eliminate. Tree search with environment feedback and frontier models remain untested. Prior tool-routing work \\citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings where individual tool calls are independent; in multi-step interactive environments, the finding is not that death exists but that the death-rate differential ($\\delta = 26.6$~pp) and its downstream effects resist all three tested intervention axes.",
          "+Three qualitatively different interventions all fail to close the gap to deterministic-level safety (16.3\\%). Retry is nearly ineffective even at $N{=}5$ trials ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$ baseline on death-prone tasks, $\\Delta = -4.5$~pp; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose-response}). Reflexion shows a steep initial decline to $34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p < 0.005$) but plateaus thereafter ($34$--$36.5\\%$ for $N \\geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$). $10{\\times}$ capability scaling reduces death rate to $34.5\\%$ (72B). That two independent mechanisms converge to nearly identical floors ($34.0\\%$ for Reflexion, $34.5\\%$ for scaling) suggests a shared structural bound set by the internal strategy's lack of ground-truth state access, not by any single intervention's limitations. All three interventions leave an ${\\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution. \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits 0\\% death across all conditions, confirming that death risk is task-specific. Compounding state estimation errors across multi-step trajectories create catastrophic failures that single-axis improvements attenuate but do not eliminate. Tree search with environment feedback and frontier models remain untested. Prior tool-routing work \\citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings where individual tool calls are independent; in multi-step interactive environments, the finding is not that death exists but that the death-rate differential ($\\delta = 26.6$~pp) and its downstream effects resist all three tested intervention axes.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}",
          " All 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \\texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \\texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}) identifies action-space grounding failure as the root cause: the majority of dying agents correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives. The dominant failure mode is not comprehension failure but inability to translate task understanding into correct actions. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \\texttt{focus on} rates (${\\sim}5\\%$; Figure~\\ref{fig:death-comparison}); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states rather than different action selection within those states. Among fatal targets, \\texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\\%), followed by \\texttt{inventory} (17.1\\%) and \\texttt{kitchen} (10.5\\%), indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling."

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DkTAYom7kXFQoquEWms8J2`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Modular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet no systematic methodology exists for diagnosing \emph{what} drives routing decisions. We introduce an episode-level counterfactual framework that decomposes routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Applied to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} arise from death asymmetry (DAF $= 90.4\%$): the internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death, while the deterministic strategy survives. Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\%), while $10{\times}$ model scaling and Reflexion each reduce death rates on death-prone tasks to ${\sim}$35\%, yet none closes the ${\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution. Cross-environment contrasts confirm framework predictions: on MATH ($N{=}795$), no routing signal emerges without catastrophic failure ($p = 0.81$).
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01P2TDJQgHu5iKLk91WheJ9C`</summary>

```text
7	\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}
8	Three qualitatively different interventions all fail to close the gap to deterministic-level safety (16.3\%). Retry is nearly ineffective even at $N{=}5$ trials ($43.5\%$ vs.\ $48.0\%$ baseline on death-prone tasks, $\Delta = -4.5$~pp; Figure~\ref{fig:dose-response}). Reflexion shows a steep initial decline to $34.0\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p < 0.005$) but plateaus thereafter ($34$--$36.5\%$ for $N \geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$). $10{\times}$ capability scaling reduces death rate to $34.5\%$ (72B). That two independent mechanisms converge to nearly identical floors ($34.0\%$ for Reflexion, $34.5\%$ for scaling) suggests a shared structural bound set by the internal strategy's lack of ground-truth state access, not by any single intervention's limitations. All three interventions leave an ${\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution. \texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits 0\% death across all conditions, confirming that death risk is task-specific. Compounding state estimation errors across multi-step trajectories create catastrophic failures that single-axis improvements attenuate but do not eliminate. Tree search with environment feedback and frontier models remain untested. Prior tool-routing work \citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings where individual tool calls are independent; in multi-step interactive environments, the finding is not that death exists but that the death-rate differential ($\delta = 26.6$~pp) and its downstream effects resist all three tested intervention axes.
9	
10	\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}
11	All 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}) identifies action-space grounding failure as the root cause: the majority of dying agents correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives. The dominant failure mode is not comprehension failure but inability to translate task understanding into correct actions. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$; Figure~\ref{fig:death-comparison}); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states rather than different action selection within those states. Among fatal targets, \texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\%), followed by \texttt{inventory} (17.1\%) and \texttt{kitchen} (10.5\%), indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling.
12	
13	\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and routing value.}
14	The oracle router matches always-deterministic on 96.3\% of decisions, improving score by only 2.89 points ($-6.58$ vs.\ $-9.47$). This \emph{oracle degeneracy} challenges a core assumption of routing research: that learning to switch between modalities yields substantial gains over any fixed strategy. When death dominance holds, the optimal policy is near-degenerate because the dominant signal (avoiding catastrophic failure) uniformly favors deterministic computation. Practitioners should first verify that the oracle margin justifies the complexity of a learned router before investing in routing infrastructure.
15	
16	\paragraph{Scaling predictions.}
17	Stronger backbones lower death rates but do not eliminate death dominance: Qwen2.5-72B reduces aggregate death from ${\sim}$51\% to ${\sim}$35\% ($-17$~pp), yet DAF remains $90.9\%$ (vs.\ $90.4\%$ at 7B), and the gap to deterministic (16.3\%) remains ${\sim}$18~pp. The Llama pilot ($\delta = +34.3\%$) versus Qwen-7B ($\delta = +26.6\%$) is consistent in the capability-increase direction. Preliminary evidence (Qwen2.5-0.5B, $N{=}15$) suggests the relationship is non-monotonic: very weak models exhibit low death rates from inability to reach dangerous states. Our results suggest that in environments where one modality triggers irreversible catastrophic failures with severe penalties, death avoidance will dominate routing signal; self-correction mechanisms can selectively reduce death-driven signal but are unlikely to eliminate death dominance.
18	
```

```text
19	\paragraph{When to use deterministic computation.}
20	Our framework provides a practical diagnostic sequence for practitioners deploying modular agents. First, identify whether the target environment contains catastrophic failure mechanisms (permadeath, irreversible penalties). Second, estimate the death-rate asymmetry between internal and deterministic strategies using a small pilot (${\sim}$50 episodes suffice for directional signal). Third, if death-asymmetry labels dominate the routing signal, a fixed deterministic strategy is likely to match or exceed a learned router at substantially lower deployment complexity. The MATH negative control ($p = 0.81$) confirms that this diagnostic correctly identifies environments where routing adds no value.
21	
22	\paragraph{DAF and framework applicability.}
23	DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}); the penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\%) is the more informative primary diagnostic. The framework requires paired episodes under two modalities, binary catastrophic outcomes, and sparse rewards. ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH lacks catastrophic outcomes (negative control); ALFWorld lacks them too, and the framework identifies information asymmetry as the alternative mechanism. When $P$ is small relative to $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$, DAF degrades (at $P{=}0$, DAF $= 7.5\%$); the framework is most informative when catastrophic penalties dominate non-death score differences. NetHack~\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, are the most direct replication targets; recent LLM agent benchmarks in these environments \citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide the infrastructure for such evaluation.
24	
```

56	
57	\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}
58	To characterize the marginal value of additional trials, we extend both Retry and Reflexion to $N{=}5$ trials on the 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$ per condition; Figure~\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat: death rate decreases from $48.0\%$ ($N{=}1$) to $43.5\%$ ($N{=}5$), a $4.5$~pp reduction across five trials. Reflexion shows a qualitatively different pattern: a steep initial decline from $47.5\%$ ($N{=}1$) to $34.0\%$ ($N{=}3$, $\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $z = 2.86$, $p < 0.005$), followed by a plateau at $34$--$36.5\%$ for $N \geq 3$ (pairwise $z$-tests $p > 0.5$, Wilson CIs overlapping). That Reflexion at $N{=}3$ ($34.0\%$) converges to nearly the same floor as $10{\times}$ capability scaling ($34.5\%$, 72B) suggests a shared structural bound: both interventions attenuate death but cannot close the ${\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution ($16.3\%$).
59	
60	\begin{figure}[t]
61	\centering
62	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}
63	\caption{Death rate as a function of trial count ($N$) for Retry and Reflexion on 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$). Retry (blue) is nearly flat; Reflexion (red) drops steeply through $N{=}3$ then plateaus. Horizontal lines: 72B scaling baseline ($34.5\%$) and deterministic baseline ($16.3\%$). Error bands: Wilson 95\% CIs.}
64	\label{fig:dose-response}
65	\end{figure}
66	
67	\subsection{Cross-Domain Negative Contrast: MATH}
68	\label{sec:math-contrast}
69	
70	On MATH Level~4--5~\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$ problems, code interpreter as deterministic modality), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy: $73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$ ($+0.5$~pp, McNemar $p = 0.81$, 95\% CI $[-2.6, +3.6]$~pp). The label distribution is balanced (107 \texttt{int\_preferred} vs.\ 95 \texttt{det\_preferred}, 74.6\% \texttt{no\_preference}), ruling out routing signal ${\geq}3.6$~pp at $\alpha{=}0.05$. Without catastrophic failure, modality routing confers no advantage.
```

```text
33	\section*{Limitations}
34	
35	\paragraph{Environmental scope.}
36	Our primary analysis uses a single environment (ScienceWorld, 11 of 30 task types) with one backbone (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct); generalization requires further validation. The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; environments with softer penalties would exhibit lower DAF values (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). The 19 excluded task types (63\%) lack routing signal because both strategies typically time out within 30 steps; increasing the step budget could surface non-death-dominated signal that our current setup does not capture. Cross-environment analyses on MATH and ALFWorld (\S\ref{sec:cross-env}, \S\ref{sec:discussion}) provide preliminary supporting evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. The Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct pilot (7 task types, $N{=}70$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}) confirms direction-consistent death patterns, though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$ scale) reduces death rate but does not eliminate death dominance (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}); validation beyond 72B remains future work.
37	
38	\paragraph{Methodological constraints.}
39	Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\citep{react-2023}. Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death rate from 42.9\% to 31.4\% (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), but more aggressive self-correction or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\citep{tot-2023} may further reduce internal death rates. Episode-level labels assign a single routing preference to entire episodes, which may be suboptimal for individual steps (particularly those preceding death events). The mislabeling fraction (episodes where the episode-level label disagrees with what step-level oracle labels would assign) is approximately 27.7\%, concentrated in death-asymmetry episodes where early steps may have favored the eventually-dying strategy. Training on pure-strategy trajectories also introduces distribution shift at deployment~\citep{dagger-2011}.
40	
41	\paragraph{Statistical limitations.}
42	Episodes within the same task type are not fully independent because they share environment dynamics, though they use different random seeds. Cluster bootstrap with $K{=}11$ task-type clusters yields wider CIs ($[73.3\%, 100\%]$); with $K < 40$ clusters, these should be interpreted cautiously~\citep{cameron-2008,webb-2023}. A wild cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$, $p < 0.001$; Appendix~\ref{app:wild-cluster}) and 100\% directional consistency across all 28 death-prone tasks (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task-ci}) mitigate this concern.
43	
44	\section*{Ethics Statement}
45	
46	This work uses ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a publicly available text-based simulation environment, and does not involve human subjects or private data. All evaluation environments (ScienceWorld, ALFWorld, MATH) are released under permissive open-source licenses (Apache 2.0 / MIT). All models used (Qwen2.5 family, Llama-3.1-8B) are publicly available for research use. All language models are accessed via standard APIs or publicly released weights. Experiments were conducted on 2$\times$ NVIDIA RTX 5090 (32GB) and 4$\times$ NVIDIA RTX 6000 PRO (96GB), totaling approximately 200 GPU-hours for data collection, router training, and evaluation. Code and data will be released upon acceptance.
47	
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly with this signal because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable routing features. A per-class confusion matrix of the 1.7B router reveals that 33\\% of steps in death episodes are mispredicted as ``internal'' (routing to the LLM in precisely the situations where it dies), with internal-class precision of only 0.14. The router's prediction rate is similar across death and non-death episodes, confirming that it cannot detect death risk from step-level features.\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nThree qualitatively different interventions all fail to close the gap to deterministic-level safety (16.3\\%). Retry is nearly ineffective even at $N{=}5$ trials ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$ baseline on death-prone tasks, $\\Delta = -4.5$~pp; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose-response}). Reflexion shows a steep initial decline to $34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p < 0.005$) but plateaus thereafter ($34$--$36.5\\%$ for $N \\geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$). $10{\\times}$ capability scaling reduces death rate to $34.5\\%$ (72B). That two independent mechanisms converge to nearly identical floors ($34.0\\%$ for Reflexion, $34.5\\%$ for scaling) suggests a shared structural bound set by the internal strategy's lack of ground-truth state access, not by any single intervention's limitations. All three interventions leave an ${\\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution. \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits 0\\% death across all conditions, confirming that death risk is task-specific. Compounding state estimation errors across multi-step trajectories create catastrophic failures that single-axis improvements attenuate but do not eliminate. Tree search with environment feedback and frontier models remain untested. Prior tool-routing work \\citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings where individual tool calls are independent; in multi-step interactive environments, the finding is not that death exists but that the death-rate differential ($\\delta = 26.6$~pp) and its downstream effects resist all three tested intervention axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \\texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \\texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}) identifies action-space grounding failure as the root cause: the majority of dying agents correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives. The dominant failure mode is not comprehension failure but inability to translate task understanding into correct actions. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \\texttt{focus on} rates (${\\sim}5\\%$; Figure~\\ref{fig:death-comparison}); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states rather than different action selection within those states. Among fatal targets, \\texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\\%), followed by \\texttt{inventory} (17.1\\%) and \\texttt{kitchen} (10.5\\%), indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and routing value.}\nThe oracle router matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions, improving score by only 2.89 points ($-6.58$ vs.\\ $-9.47$). This \\emph{oracle degeneracy} challenges a core assumption of routing research: that learning to switch between modalities yields substantial gains over any fixed strategy. When death dominance holds, the optimal policy is near-degenerate because the dominant signal (avoiding catastrophic failure) uniformly favors deterministic computation. Practitioners should first verify that the oracle margin justifies the complexity of a learned router before investing in routing infrastructure.\n\n\\paragraph{Scaling predictions.}\nStronger backbones lower death rates but do not eliminate death dominance: Qwen2.5-72B reduces aggregate death from ${\\sim}$51\\% to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($-17$~pp), yet DAF remains $90.9\\%$ (vs.\\ $90.4\\%$ at 7B), and the gap to deterministic (16.3\\%) remains ${\\sim}$18~pp. The Llama pilot ($\\delta = +34.3\\%$) versus Qwen-7B ($\\delta = +26.6\\%$) is consistent in the capability-increase direction. Preliminary evidence (Qwen2.5-0.5B, $N{=}15$) suggests the relationship is non-monotonic: very weak models exhibit low death rates from inability to reach dangerous states. Our results suggest that in environments where one modality triggers irreversible catastrophic failures with severe penalties, death avoidance will dominate routing signal; self-correction mechanisms can selectively reduce death-driven signal but are unlikely to eliminate death dominance.\n\n\\paragraph{When to use deterministic computation.}\nOur framework provides a practical diagnostic sequence for practitioners deploying modular agents. First, identify whether the target environment contains catastrophic failure mechanisms (permadeath, irreversible penalties). Second, estimate the death-rate asymmetry between internal and deterministic strategies using a small pilot (${\\sim}$50 episodes suffice for directional signal). Third, if death-asymmetry labels dominate the routing signal, a fixed deterministic strategy is likely to match or exceed a learned router at substantially lower deployment complexity. The MATH negative control ($p = 0.81$) confirms that this diagnostic correctly identifies environments where routing adds no value.\n\n\\paragraph{DAF and framework applicability.}\nDAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}); the penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the more informative primary diagnostic. The framework requires paired episodes under two modalities, binary catastrophic outcomes, and sparse rewards. ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH lacks catastrophic outcomes (negative control); ALFWorld lacks them too, and the framework identifies information asymmetry as the alternative mechanism. When $P$ is small relative to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$, DAF degrades (at $P{=}0$, DAF $= 7.5\\%$); the framework is most informative when catastrophic penalties dominate non-death score differences. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, are the most direct replication targets; recent LLM agent benchmarks in these environments \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide the infrastructure for such evaluation.\n",

  "new_string": "That Reflexion at $N{=}3$ ($34.0\\%$) converges to nearly the same floor as $10{\\times}$ capability scaling ($34.5\\%$, 72B) suggests a shared structural bound: both interventions attenuate death but cannot close the ${\\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution ($16.3\\%$). The contrast illuminates the mechanism: Retry's per-trial conditional death rate remains constant at ${\\sim}48\\%$, confirming that the agent does not learn between blind retries. Reflexion's per-trial rate declines from $47.5\\%$ to ${\\sim}31\\%$ at Trial~3 before a modest uptick at Trials~4--5; this non-monotonicity is a descriptive observation (Wilson CIs overlap across all $N \\geq 3$ conditions) rather than evidence of over-reflexion degradation."
}

          " We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance. Retry gives the agent three attempts per episode without self-correction; Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} adds verbal self-reflection between trials (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\\% vs.\\ 42.9\\% baseline; the 2.0~pp difference is within sampling noise, Fisher exact $p > 0.6$). Reflexion reduces death to 31.4\\% by rescuing 29.3\\% of first-trial deaths (27 of 92), but the gap to deterministic (16.3\\%) remains 15.1~pp. Trial~1 death rate under Reflexion (41.8\\%) matches the single-trial baseline; the reduction comes from cross-trial self-correction, not from the Reflexion prompt itself. Trial~3 adds minimal rescue beyond Trial~2 (net $+4$). Scaling from 7B to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$ parameters) moderately reduces aggregate death rate from ${\\sim}$51\\% to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp) across death-prone tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}), but does not approach deterministic-level safety (16.3\\%). Task-level variation ranges from $+5$~pp (chemistry-mix, where 72B performs slightly worse) to $-31$~pp (lifespan-longest-lived). This variation suggests that scaling yields large reductions in biological-reasoning tasks ($-15$ to $-31$~pp) but provides no benefit for chemical state tracking (chemistry-mix: $+5$~pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}). The 31.4\\% in Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement} covers all 11 tasks; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\\sim}$35\\%, the figure reported in the Abstract. Recomputing DAF with 72B as the internal strategy ($N{=}220$) yields $90.9\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[83.9\\%, 97.3\\%]$), nearly identical to the 7B value ($90.4\\%$); the death-asymmetry label fraction rises from $78.9\\%$ (7B) to $84.3\\%$ (72B). On death-prone tasks ($N{=}200$), DAF reaches $99.5\\%$ ($[98.0\\%, 100.2\\%]$) with $95.9\\%$ of labels driven by death asymmetry. A matched 10-task comparison confirms scale-independence: 8 of 10 death-prone tasks maintain DAF $= 100\\%$ at both 7B and 72B; episode-weighted DAF declines only from $100\\%$ to $97.5\\%$. No task shifts from death-dominated to performance-dominated at $10{\\times}$ scale. Scaling lowers death rate but leaves death dominance structurally intact.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}",
          "-To characterize the marginal value of additional trials, we extend both Retry and Reflexion to $N{=}5$ trials on the 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$ per condition; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose-response}). Reflexion shows a qualitatively different pattern: a steep initial decline from $47.5\\%$ ($N{=}1$) to $34.0\\%$ ($N{=}3$, $\\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $z = 2.86$, $p < 0.005$), followed by a plateau at $34$--$36.5\\%$ for $N \\geq 3$ (pairwise $z$-tests $p > 0.5$, Wilson CIs overlapping). 43	\midrule
44	Single-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\
45	Retry ($3{\times}$) & 40.9 & 220 \\
46	Reflexion ($3{\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\
47	Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$ scale) & 34.5$^\dagger$ & 200 \\
48	Deterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\
49	\bottomrule
50	\end{tabular}
51	\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under agent improvement methods. $\dagger$\,72B death rate is the aggregate over 200 episodes across 10 death-prone tasks; \texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded (0\% death at both scales). Reflexion partially reduces death but the gap to deterministic remains large.}
52	\label{tab:agent-improvement}
53	\end{table}
54	
55	We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance. Retry gives the agent three attempts per episode without self-correction; Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} adds verbal self-reflection between trials (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\% vs.\ 42.9\% baseline; the 2.0~pp difference is within sampling noise, Fisher exact $p > 0.6$). Reflexion reduces death to 31.4\% by rescuing 29.3\% of first-trial deaths (27 of 92), but the gap to deterministic (16.3\%) remains 15.1~pp. Trial~1 death rate under Reflexion (41.8\%) matches the single-trial baseline; the reduction comes from cross-trial self-correction, not from the Reflexion prompt itself. Trial~3 adds minimal rescue beyond Trial~2 (net $+4$). Scaling from 7B to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$ parameters) moderately reduces aggregate death rate from ${\sim}$51\% to ${\sim}$35\% ($\Delta \approx -17$~pp) across death-prone tasks (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}), but does not approach deterministic-level safety (16.3\%). Task-level variation ranges from $+5$~pp (chemistry-mix, where 72B performs slightly worse) to $-31$~pp (lifespan-longest-lived). This variation suggests that scaling yields large reductions in biological-reasoning tasks ($-15$ to $-31$~pp) but provides no benefit for chemical state tracking (chemistry-mix: $+5$~pp; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}). The 31.4\% in Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement} covers all 11 tasks; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\sim}$35\%, the figure reported in the Abstract. Recomputing DAF with 72B as the internal strategy ($N{=}220$) yields $90.9\%$ (95\% CI: $[83.9\%, 97.3\%]$), nearly identical to the 7B value ($90.4\%$); the death-asymmetry label fraction rises from $78.9\%$ (7B) to $84.3\%$ (72B). On death-prone tasks ($N{=}200$), DAF reaches $99.5\%$ ($[98.0\%, 100.2\%]$) with $95.9\%$ of labels driven by death asymmetry. A matched 10-task comparison confirms scale-independence: 8 of 10 death-prone tasks maintain DAF $= 100\%$ at both 7B and 72B; episode-weighted DAF declines only from $100\%$ to $97.5\%$. No task shifts from death-dominated to performance-dominated at $10{\times}$ scale. Scaling lowers death rate but leaves death dominance structurally intact.
56	
```

Scaling from 7B to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$ parameters) moderately reduces aggregate death rate from ${\\sim}$51\\% to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp) across death-prone tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}), but does not approach deterministic-level safety (16.3\\%). This variation suggests that scaling yields large reductions in biological-reasoning tasks ($-15$ to $-31$~pp) but provides no benefit for chemical state tracking (chemistry-mix: $+5$~pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}).           " \\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}",
          " To characterize the marginal value of additional trials, we extend both Retry and Reflexion to $N{=}5$ trials on the 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$ per condition; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat: death rate decreases from $48.0\\%$ ($N{=}1$) to $43.5\\%$ ($N{=}5$), a $4.5$~pp reduction across five trials. Reflexion shows a qualitatively different pattern: a steep initial decline from $47.5\\%$ ($N{=}1$) to $34.0\\%$ ($N{=}3$, $\\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $z = 2.86$, $p < 0.005$), followed by a plateau at $34$--$36.5\\%$ for $N \\geq 3$ (pairwise $z$-tests $p > 0.5$, Wilson CIs overlapping). <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01NgPFrAz1YoDWAYERgUnZyf`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Modular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet no systematic methodology exists for diagnosing \emph{what} drives routing decisions. We introduce an episode-level counterfactual framework that decomposes routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Applied to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} arise from death asymmetry (DAF $= 90.4\%$): the internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death, while the deterministic strategy survives. Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\%), while $10{\times}$ model scaling and Reflexion each reduce death rates on death-prone tasks to ${\sim}$35\%, yet none closes the ${\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution. Cross-environment contrasts confirm framework predictions: on MATH ($N{=}795$), no routing signal emerges without catastrophic failure ($p = 0.81$).
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nModular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet no systematic methodology exists for diagnosing \\emph{what} drives routing decisions. We introduce an episode-level counterfactual framework that decomposes routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Applied to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} arise from death asymmetry (DAF $= 90.4\\%$): the internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death, while the deterministic strategy survives. Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\\%), while $10{\\times}$ model scaling and Reflexion each reduce death rates on death-prone tasks to ${\\sim}$35\\%, yet none closes the ${\\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution. Cross-environment contrasts confirm framework predictions: on MATH ($N{=}795$), no routing signal emerges without catastrophic failure ($p = 0.81$).\n\\end{abstract}\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01LjbmaTgt9YP8KezqoxXe9y`</summary>

```text
1	# Retry Dose-Response Per-Trial Analysis
2	
3	## Death Definition
4	- is_death = (score <= -100)
5	- Episode-level DR(N) = P(all first N trials are death)
6	- Each trial is a complete restart (retry: no reflection; reflexion: with reflection)
7	
8	## Dose-Response Table (death-prone tasks, 200 episodes)
9	
10	| N | Retry DR | 95% CI | Reflexion DR | 95% CI |
11	|---|----------|--------|--------------|--------|
12	| 1 | 0.4650 | [0.397, 0.534] | 0.4600 | [0.392, 0.529] |
13	| 2 | 0.4450 | [0.378, 0.514] | 0.3400 | [0.278, 0.408] |
14	| 3 | 0.4150 | [0.349, 0.484] | 0.2700 | [0.213, 0.335] |
15	
16	## Key Findings
17	- Retry N=1→N=3: only -5pp (flat curve, ~2pp/trial marginal improvement)
18	- Reflexion N=1→N=3: -19pp (steep curve, reflection mechanism is key)
19	- N=1 baselines nearly identical (~46%), confirming same starting point
20	- Blind retry ineffective for death avoidance; reflexion's benefit comes from self-correction, not retry opportunity
21	
22	## Source
23	- Data: results/retry_dose_response_analysis.json on westb-14838
24	- Method: Extracted from existing retry-control-baseline and reflexion per-trial JSON data
25	- No new GPU experiments needed for N=1,2,3
```
